THE   FOURTH   PART

Till', descent of tlio Gounicvs of Somersetshire is discoverable by trneiim,-
the successive lords of tlie manors of Barew-Gurney and Inglislicombe, in
that county.

NiL!;ellus licld tliese manors at the Survey under the Bishop of Coutances.^
In tlie ICxon Domesday, \vliich was compiled from the same orii^inal rolls
as the great Domesday, but at greater length, this Nigellus is called
Nigellus de Gurnai,'' and was undoid:)tedly the ancestor of tliis branch of
the Gonrnays. There is no proof of the degree of relationship in which
he stood to the Norman Barons of Gournay, but we think there can be
little doubt of his beiuLC of the same race. Three of that family are
deseril)ed as ])eing jiresent at the battle of Hastings ; <^ whom we take to
l)c this Nigellus de Gom-nay, together with the elder Mugh de Gournay
(II.) and his son Hugh (III.) It is very possible Nigellus might be
another son of the elder Hugh.

Hawisa de Gournay possessed the manors of Barew and Inglishcombe
in the reigns of Stephen and Henry II.; and in a deed of hers preserved
in Madox's Formulare Anglicanum, she mentions her father Robert de
Gournay.''    This Robert we presume to be the son of Nigellus.

It is worthy of remark that Hawisa de Gournay, although thrice married,
always retained her paternal surname of Gournay. Her first husband, by
whom she had a son who died under age and unmarried, was Roger de
Baalun ; her second, as we believe, was Robert Fitz Harding, by whom she
had an only daughter Eva de Gournay, wife of Thomas, son of William de

## p. 593 (#667) ############################################

593

NIGEL DE GOURNAY

HF.T^n the manors of Harcw and Inglescoinbe, or Englisliconibo, at the time
of the Survey, under the Bishop of Coutances. Besides the above, he held
under tlie same tlic manors of Wyche, Caivel, and Tvviverton, and, jointly
with ludcran, jiart of Bacoile (Backwell),* all in Somersetshire. In the
preat Domesday he is simply called Nigellus ; but in the Exon Domesday,
vrhieh is supposed to be cojiied from the original rolls from which some
counties in the great Domesday were compiled,'^ he is called Nigellus de
(iurnai and de Gurnaio. (Ajipendix CI.) l-'rom these circumstances, and
the fact that these manors of Barrow and Inglishcombe descended to the
Somersetshire (Journays, there can be no doubt this Nigel de Gournai
was the original ancestor of that family : what his connection with the
Norman Barons might be is not traceable. In a catalogue of the Normans
present at the battle of Hastings by M. du Moulin, by collating the MS.
of M. Duchesne with another in the Hotel de \\\\e at Rouen, three indi-
viduals of the Gurnai family are given by name:"

" Le Sieur de Brai, le comte.
" Le Seigneur de Gournai."
It is probable these three were Hugh de Gournay II, his son Hugh III.,
who married Basilia Flaitel, and this Nigel de Gournai, who might possibly
be another son of Hugh. The manor of EngHshcombe, or Ingliscombe,
is situated about three miles from Bath, upon a ridge of high hills, and
was, according to tradition, formerly the seat of some of the Saxon kings.
The ancient castle of the Gournais stood on a break of the hill, about a
quarter of a mile east of the village. It was surrounded by a deep foss,
which is still remaining, as are also some small parts of wall work on the
south and west sides ; but the building itself has been demolished nearly a
couple of centuries, and the rectorial barn near the church erected with

Dorar?day, vol. i. pp. 88 and 89. '' Reports of Record Conimissioners, vol.

Du Moulin, Ilistoire de Normandie, p. 185.

Geoffrey Bishop of Coutances, under whom Nigel de Gournay held these
manors, was a man of noble Norman extraction, and accompanied the
Con(iueror to England. He and Odo Bishop of Bayeaux were the two
prelates who most distinguished themselves in this expedition. Geoffrey
Bishop of Coutances was of the family of Moiithray ; he was a great military
leader, being, as Orderic Vital says,'' more skilful in arms than divinity ;
knowing better to train soldiers than to instruct his clergy.    The Conqueror

## p. 598 (#672) ############################################

598

ROBERT DE GOURNAY I.

Is the next of this line that we have heen able to traee. lie is mentioned
in a deed of Hawisa de Gournai his daughter, lady of the before-mentioned
manors of Inglishcombe and Barew ; in this deed it is stated that he gave
land in Cliveware to Alexander de Bidecumb." (App. CV.) He also
oecurs as witness to the deed of Roger de Baalun, husband of his daughter
Hawisa de Gournay, whereby Roger gave to the monks of Bermondsey
land in Upperton, &c. Jn this latter instrument he is called Dominus
Robertas de Gurnai, whence we conclude he was a knight. Roger his
brother is the next witness in this deed.''    (Ap]). CII.)

Hawisa, the daughter of this Robert de Gournai, lived in the reigns of
Stephen and Henry n.*"; so that it seems extremely probal)le that he and
Roger de Gournai his brother were sons of the preceding Nigel. Indeed I
am disposed to believe this Robert de Gournay is the same person as
Robertus filius Nigelli (Robert Fitz-Nigel), mentioned in the Pipe Roll of
the 31st Henry I. (page T"], edition ))y the Rev. Joseph Hunter,) as holding
lands in Gloucestershire in custody for William de Fescamp, a minor,
which William de Fescamp was a considerable benefactor to the abbey of
Beaubec, founded by Hugh de Gournay IV.; and amongst other donations
William de Fescamp gave lands in England, which he held of Robert,
surnamed the Consul, Earl of Gloucester. Now we find in the Liber
Niger, which was written in 1 167, that Robert de Gournay had held nine
knights' fees of the Earl of Gloucester ; and I t'' srefore conjecture that
Robert Fitz-Nigel and Robert de Gournay are ^ e same person ; and, if
this is a correct inference, Robert is proved to be the son of Nigel de
Gournay.

Madox, Formularc Anglicanum, No. 100, p. 54.

## p. 599 (#673) ############################################

A. n.   1131.] ROnEHT FITZ-NIGRL. 599

The following are the extracts from the original documents relating to
this point:—

From the Pipe Roll, 31 Hen. I.—" Willelmus de Fiscampo debet centum
marcas argenti pro terra patris sui, quam Rohcrtvs Filius Nigelli tenehat."

iM-om the foundation ('hartcr of the Priory of Beaubec, page 106 of this
Record.—" Terram quoque quam prefatus Willelmus (de Fiscanno) vobis
dedir in Anglia apudChenobrotonum," quam Robertus Gloucestrie Consul,
e cujus foodo erat, vobis concessit."

From the Liber Niger (edition by Hearne, vol. i. p. Kil), under the fiefs
of William Earl of Gloucester, son and successor of Earl Robert (the
Consul.)—"Feodum quod fuit Roberti de Gornaco, ix milites."

By this it appears these fiefs'' had formerly been Robert de Gournay's,
who was probaljly dead before the year 11G/, when the Liber Niger was
compiled, being the register of the aid granted by the tenants of the
crown upon the occasion of the marriage of Matilda, daughter of King
Henry IL with the Duke of Saxony.

From these documents it is highly probable that Robert de Gournay
and Robert Fitz-Nigel are the same person.

## p. 600 (#674) ############################################

600

HAWISA  DR CnUUNAl,

Dai'ghtf.u of Unliert de Gournny, (App. VX.) and Lady of llic manors of
Barew and Ingliplicond^c. She is stated by Diiadale -'' to have frivcn to the
abliey of Bcnnondsey the chnrcli of InijHsheomhe, in tlie year 1112, I'Jth
Henry I. This date is given to the above gift in tlie Annales Abbatire de
Bermondsey, IMS. Ilarl. '231. fol. 10 ; l)ut is certainly erroneons, as Patrick
Earl of Salisbury witnessed the deed of gift of Ilawisa, of tlie said churcli
of IngH-hcomlie, and he flourished from 11.')2 to 1167.'' I transcribe tlie
original deed from the extracts of the Bermondsey chartulary preserved in
the Cotton MS. Claud. A. vni. fol. 11,'',.

"Ego Ilawis' de Gurnai pro amore Dei &c. et pro anima Rogeri de
Baalun mariti mci et Rogeri Baabin fiHi mei ^:c. dedi monachis de
Bermondsey Ecclesiam de Ingliscumbe, Test. Patricius Comes, Radiis
Baalun, Hamelinus de Baalun, jVrnoldus de Baalun, Ilobertus de
Gundcville, Mattus de Giirnay, Humfus de Clunn, Petr\is filius ejus, Almerus
filius Gaufri, &c."

We must remark upon this charter that the fact of Matthew de Gournay
of the Norfolk line of the Gournays (see page 301,) being a witness to this
and to other charters of Hawisa de Gournay, (App. CI I. and CV.) is a
strong presumptive evidence of the Somersetshire Gournays being of the
same stock as those of Norfolk and Normandy.

This Hawisa de Gournai was a great heiress, and a lady of extensive
estates: so much so that she not only retained her own paternal surname,
although thrice married, but transmitted it to her descendants even
in the female line,' and with it these manors, of he original possession of
the (jlournays in Somersetshire, together with va-.ous others which she held
in capita of the Crown.

Monasticon, New Edit. vol. v. p. 88. '' Dugdale's Bar., vol.

History of the House of Ivory, vol. ii. p. 479.

## p. 601 (#675) ############################################

A.  n.   1184.] ROGER DE  BAALUN. GO I

Tlie ret(Mition of the paternal surname by women of large inheritance
was conunon during the i)eriod of our Anglo-Norman kings. The Normans
introduced into tliis country the system of hereditary surnames, and their
descendants were always extremely tenacious of the high distinction of
such as marked tlieir Norman extraction. That Ilawisa dc Curnai married
Roger de Eaalun, is proved, not merely l)y tlie deed we liave already
(juoted, hut by various otliers. lie was son of \Viiu>bnId or (jlrimbaldus
de Baalun, of a family wlio came to England at the Concpiest' By him
she had a son Roger de Haalun.

Roger, the husl)and of Hawisa, gave to the abliey of Bcrmondsey lands in
Upton ; and Roger her son before his deatli Ix-cjueatlied to the same
religious house " centum solidatas terr?e," land worth a hundred solidataj
rent, of which the attestation of Hawisa his mother, and Patrit'k Earl of
Salisl)ury, still exists in the Bcrmondsey Chartulary. This nolilcman was
guardian of the lands of the younger Roger de Baalun, and deposited the
deed of legacy on the high altar of St. Savioiu* in Bcrmondsey. (App. CII.)

It seems that Hawisa had no other child by Roger de Baalun, as the
inheritance of that family, U])ton, Hardwick, and other places, devolved on
Henry of Newmarch, nephew of the elder Roger de Baalun.''

The Pijie Rolls of the ^Oth, 'J7th, 28th, 29th, 30th, and 31st years of
Henry U. show Hawisa to have had a jointure in Horsington in Somerset-
shire ; and as this manor was afterwards in the family of Newmarch,*^ who
inherited feifs of the De Baaluns, there can be no doubt that it was the
jointure settled on her by her husband, Roger de Baalun.

She held an interest in dower in the manor of Lesham in Hampshire ;
which she gave to Engelram de Abernon, who sub-enfeoffed his brothers,
each in a third part of it.''    (App. CIV.)

This dower in Lesham was from Roger de Clere, who was probably her
second husband. This appears from the Lewes Chartulary, whii ^ contains
the following charter, proving this : " Hawisia de Gurnais, salute.n, sciatis
me eoncessisse monachis de Lewes totam terram de Athelingworth, quam

602 HAWTSA nn gournay. [paut iv.

in dotem acccpi ex doiiatione Rogeri de Clcra, mariti mei, sieut Rogerus de
Clera et Radulphus frater et ha.>res ipsius dedcrunt."

It appears by this that Hawisa liad no issue by Roger dr ('lore, as Ralf
bis brother was liis heir. Atbeliiigworth is one of tlie manors or divisions
of Brighton.^

Hawisa de (;urnai, it seems, remarried Robert I'itz-IIarding, and liad l)y
him an only ebild, Eva de Gournay, wlio marrierl Thomas tlie son of
WiUiam de Ilarptree. The issue of Tliomas de llarptree and Eva de
Gournay was, as we shall hereafter see, Robert de Gournay, a great Baron
who lived in the reign of Henry III. and who was, in the male line, the
fomider of the Baronial family of the Somersetshire Gournays.

It is extremely probable that Robert Fitz-Harding. and his wife Hawisa
de Gournay, were the founders of the nunnery of Minchin-Barrow, within
the lordship of Barrow-Gourney. Leland implies that one of the Gournays
foimded tliat house ;'' and CoUinson, in his History of Somersetshire, states
that one of the Fitz-Hardings was the founder/

This religious foundation is mentioned in the will of Hugh Bishop of
Lineoln in 1211;'' it was therefore in existence at that time.

Hawisa de Gournay by deed (App. CV.) enfeoffed Thomas the son of
William de Harptree of lands in Barew-Gournay, which the said Thomas
bought for 103 solidi of Alexander de Bidecumb, who acknowledged him-
self satisfied in the jiresence of Hawisa and her men at her court at Barew.
This land, which had been given by Robert de Gurnai father of Hawisa,
the said Alexander de Bidecuml) surrendered by presenting Hawisa, as
lady of the manor, with the branch of a tree, which she presented to
Thomas de Harptree in token of his being in possession of the said lands ;
and Thomas gave her as a token in return a golden ring.^

This Thomas was husband of Eva de Gournay, the daughter, as we
believe, of Hawisa de Gournay and Robert Fitz-Harding.

The last mention of Hawisa is in the Pipe Roll 34 Henry II. 1188 ; she

patronymic of his own father, in ])refercnce to
tracing the further descent; and this was a
common practice, and led to the formation of such
surnames as Fitz-Walter and Fitz-IIcrbert.

## p. 614 (#688) ############################################

ROBKRT  HE COURNAY TI.

Son and licir of I^va de fJournay and Thonia'^ do Ilarptrce. lie inliorited
from his mother tlie manors of Inglislicombe and Harow-Gournay ; and,
through her, from Maurice de Gaunt his uncle, tlie inheritance of Robert
I'itz-Harding. their father, viz. Were, Beverstone, King's Weston, &c.; and
from his fatlier the inheritance of the llarptrees, Harptree, Farrington-
Gurney, ^c.., and Cernecote, in Wiltshire, which was the manor belonging
to Matilda, the mother of Thomas de Harptree.

From all these properties centering in Robert de Gournay, he became
enfeoffed of very extensive possessions. His assumption of the name of
de Gournay is a curious instance of the manner in which surnames were
adopted at the period in which he lived.

His reason for calling himself de Gournay must have been the circum-
stance of the family of Harptree being in fact without a surname, which
appears by all their deeds, and most of the entries in pipe rolls and fines.
The same observation would apply to the Fitz-Hardings; so that there is
no doubt he chose the appellation of de Gournav as being more honourable
in his day, inasmuch as it was a name of Norman origin, and was become
a fixed api)ellative.

In the Hritish Museum is an original letter of Robert de Gournay," of
which we give an engraved fac-simile.    The subject of it is merely to call

I I ~r~i I I together a certain number of witnesses to meet for
I 1 the attestation of a  deed  to be executed by  him ;   it

LI LI Ll is, in fact, only a sort of circular to the parties re-
quested to attend, and was doubtless sent round from
house to house by a messenger. His seal appended
to it is, Paly of six, the bearing of the Gournays of
Somersetshire, with a label  as eldest son ;  so  that it

This document, translated, is as follows:—

" Robert de Gurnay to William Tilly, Thomas de IMorton, John de
Farinston, llcnry de Birknoller,'William'of the Bourne, Lawrence late
Provost of Wells, Robert Fitzwalter of Chedder, and William his brother,
William de Clewer, greeting. I beseech you that you may be witnesses
for me when I shall make an exchange with the monks of Brewerne for
that part of Ilydon which the Templars hold of me, which (to wit,) part
these monks had formerly held of me in farm.    Farewell."

Bruerne, or Brueria abbey, is in Oxfordshire, and amongst its bene-
factors was William Fitz-^Villiam Fitz-,Tohn, who ccave lands in Ilarpetree
and Priddy,  in Somersetshire, and who was father of Thomas (de llarpe-

## p. 616 (#691) ############################################

nin ROnRRT DE GOUnNAY  II. [pART IV.

tree), Robert de Gournay's father ;  so that Hydon was probably a part of
one of those parishes."

This Robert may be considered as the founder of tlie male Hne of tlie
Rarons de (lournay, in Somersetshire. These barons were lords of the
fourth part of Mendip, according to Lcland.'' Amontrst their i)rincipal
castles was that of Beverstone, in Gloucestershire, the fine ruins of which
now exist; but it was considerably enlarged by Thomas Lord Berkeley, to
whom it was sold, and who laid out upon it the ransom he had obtained
for prisoners taken by him in the battle of Poictiers.'^

Richemounte Castle, at East Harptree, was another of tlicir feudal
residences. Leland says,"* "Gurney usyd to ly much at Richemonte
castle. It stondithe in the rote of Mendipe, este from Bristow, in the
paroche of Este Harptre, by the paroche churche of it. There standeth
yet a pece of the dungeon of it.    Sir John Newton d\'gged up many old

foundations of it toward buyldynge of a new house hard thereby, caullyd

Estewood."

"There  is   a  nother village  by Est Harptre,  caulyd West Harptre-

Gurney; and there be the variete of arms that Gurney gave in the glasse

wyndowes, and his cote armure."

" At  such time as Gurney lived,  the Lord  Fitzwarine  was master of

Mondepe Forest, by inheritance, and it was well furnished with dcre ; but

now, after for riots and trespassys done in huntynge, it was deforestyd,

and so yet remayneth."

Attain.' " Tliere was a, t^^oodly castle at Estewood, called Richemnnte,
where noble Guriiey lay much. Yt is now defaced to the hard ground ;
and Sir John Newton, now lord of it, hath made his house of the ruins
thereof, yn the very place wher the Graunire of Riehmont C'astelle was in
Gurney's tyine." This is the account of Leland, who wrote in the reii^n of
Henry VIII.

CoUinson, in his History of Somersetshire, thus mentions this castle :''
" Richraont Castle, the old fortress of the Harptrees and Gournays, lies
about lialf a mile north-westward from the church. In 1 138 it was garri-
soned by Sir William de Harptree in favour of Maude the Empress, but
King Stephen got possession of it by stratagem ; for many years after this
the castle continued in preservation. It was an irregular fortification;
the approach to it from the south-west. The vestiges of the dungeon, a
circular building, are still visible. On the north and east sides is a steep
d(>sccnt into a deep narrow winding glen ; the sides of which are deeply
veiled with wood, and very romantic."

In the 9th of Homy III. (,122.5,) n tine was levied htlweon Robert
de Gournay and his grandfather \Villiani, son of Jnlin (de ] larptrce),
respecting tlie manor of Kerenton, \\hicii was settled npon William. The
tine states it was " de hereditate Matildis quondam nxoris ipsius NVillelmi,
esse jus ipsius Iloberti, ut illius cpii fuit filius I'home primogeniti tilii
il)sorum Willehni et Matildis, cni Thome idem Willelmus prius con-
eesserat et heredibus suis ex Eva uxore sua matre ejusdem Robert!
genitis," &c.

The office copy of this fine is given in App. CVII. No. I.

In tl\e same reign he obtained the grant of a fair at his manor of ^Vere.

The 25th of Henry III. (1240), Robert de Gournay paid twenty pounds
to the King for being excused attending him at that time into Gascony ;«
and in the 41st of the same King (125G), had summons to be at Bristol,
in the octaves of St. Peter ad vincula, well furnished with horse and arms,
to march with him into Wales ; and in the same way, 42nd Henry III.
(1257), to be at Chester; and in the 47th (12G2) at Hereford, to restrain
the incursions of the W^elsh.''

Robert de Gournay confirmed the advowson of the chin-cli of Tnglisli-
combe to the monks of Bermondsey by deed. This was before tlie I 1th
of Henry III. (1227), when a fine was levied between him and Hugh,
Prior of Bermondsey, in further confirmation of it.    (App. CVH. No. II.)

Robert de Gournay was either founder or i)rineipal benefactor of the
hospital, called Gaunt's Hospital, in Bristol.*^ This hospital was dedicated
to the Virgin and St. Mark, and was situated in the ancient manor of
IMlleswyck, which now forms part of the city of Bristol. The nature of
tlie foundation very much resembled that of Saint Cross, near Winchester,
(as in both a certain number of poor persons were fed daily) according to
the foundation charters. Some accounts state that Robert Fitz-Harding
or de Were, the grandfather of Robert de Gournay, was the original
founder of this religious house, on the site  of his own residence ; others,

He gives for his own soul, and that of Maurice de CJaunt his uncle,
and those of his ancestors and successors, his manor of Faulet, for the
support of a master and three chaplains and the relief of one hundred poor
persons daily ; he also gave other estates and houses in Bristol, reserving
to himself and heirs the right of being entertained at the hospital according
to their convenience. He also desires that each of the hundred poor
persons should receive a loaf of forty-five solid! weight, with sufficient
jiottaije prcparcfl from oatnu'al; and in making the bread he directs an
e(pial (juantity of the flour of l)eans and of barley, or fine wheat, shovild
be used.

To this charter the first witness is Ralf de Neville, Bishop of Chichester,
which fixes the date of it after the year 1223.

This deed was afterwards confirmed by a fine between Robert de
Govu-nai and flenry Gaunt, master of the hospital, in 1238.*

hood he ,hall have the shield only fixed, which

" Any person after admission and within tlie
time of probation, if he should be fonnd not fit,
may depart or lie removed by the master. In
fasting and other things to be observed by the
members of tliis house, let it be according to
the custom of the friers of the hospital of Lech-
lade ; but in divine offices according to the
custom and order of Sarum. In burying the
dead, whetlur ]irince or prelate be sent for
burial, tlie said ciiaplains and clerks are to wear
the habit of the said hospital, or in their more
solemn apparel, according to the custom of
Sarum, may meet the same, provided the said
habit is not used elsewhere but in the choir,
or elsewhere when free from ecclesiastical oftice.

" As to mass and its solemnities the said
chaplain and clerks are to observe the follow-
ing rules, viz. one mass shall be celebrated in
the morning for the Blessed Virgin Mary, the
second for the dead, and the third for the day :
this to be coutiiuied ev(!ry day ; the other chap-
lains may celebrate mass for the living and the
dead,   and  chiefly   for  the  benefactors   of the

house, at tlie discretion of the master. Divine
service being endi'd, two chaplains, and the
aforesaid six clerks, wearing the badge of the
house, with two lay-brolhers, e.i.l, v.ith a little
knife in his hand, shall cut tlu- bread for the
impotent and weak, who are to be sirved to
their will between one and thive, bi'fure the
chaplains and clerks shall dine; that receiving
their prescribed portion there, they may never-
theless get elsewliere what is necessary for them.
'• The master, cha],lain, clerks, and the
bretliieii bearing their habit, may sleep in one
house, and may eat and drink in the dining-
room, but no secular [icrson shall eat there or
any where within the bounds of the hospital
unless by special leave of the master, or de-
tained there by sickness, when lie must be
refreshed in the infirmary. It' any stranger
shall make a visit to the master, he may be at
liberty to dine in his chamber or elsewhere at
his choice; but then he is to have one or two
of the aforesaid chaplains at table with him.
If the said master shall dine out of the refec-
tory, or lie out of his bedchamber, or travel
abroail, whether within or out of the town of
liristol, one or two chaplains are to be with
him, first ap]ioiuting one of the chaplains or
brethren of the order to oflliciato in his stead.
No chaplain, clerk, or brother shall eat or
drink out of his house in the same town unless
in the presence of his bishop or patron, or in
religious houses, nor without consent of the
master or his vicegerent, and then some of the
brethren in their habit shall be with him, least
any of them should be seen wandering abroad
alone, in the town, out of the precincts of the
said house ; and at table the master and chap-
lains shall use only black mantles and black
cowls, but elsewhere they shall have the arms
of   the   house   outermost,   gules,   three   geese

636

JOHN DE GOURNAY OF EAST HARPTREE AND HIS
DESCENDANTS.

John de Gournay, eldest son and heir of Ansehn de Gournay and
Sibilla de Byvon, was twenty-six years old at his father's death, in I'JHG ;
and had livery of his land tlie following year :" so that he was born in
1 •_>(•)(). In 1280, the !»th Edw. I. when he was twenty years of age, he was
already married to Olivia daughter of Henry Lord Luvcl of Kari, and
sister of Hugh Luvel, Baron of Kari, who agreed in that year to make
payment quarterly until all was discharged of 100 marks, her marriage
portion; but, as their daughter Elizabeth was sixteen years old in 1'291,
they must have married about the year 1275, when John de Gournay was
only fifteen years old.

The family of the Lords Luvel of Castle Gary, in Somersetshire, were
descended from William son of Ascelin Gouel de Percheval, Lord of Ivery
in Normandy,'' from whom as we have already seen the family of De
Harptree was derived, according to the History of the House of Ivery;
hut we have shown that this descent of the Harptrees is erroneous, and
that they are derived from the house of Tilly in Normandy (Apj). GVI.)
The Lovds were Barons of great estate, and became extinct in the reign of
Edward III.

The arms of Luvel of Castle Gary were, Or, semee of
cross-crosslets, a lion rampant azure.

A branch of this family seated at Minster Lovel in
Oxfordshire has been already noticed in this Record,
page 190.

In 15 Edward I. 1287, John de Gournay, son and heir
of Ansehn, confirmed to the monks of St. Peter's,
Gloucester, certain lands, and the advowson  of Bevers-

Kot. Tin. 15 Edw. I. m. 7.

Ord. Vit. 596 A.

The only child and heiress of John de Gournay and Olivia his wife was
Elizabeth, wlio was sixteen years of age at her father's death, and was then
married to Jt)lni A])-.\dam, who doing his fealty, had livery of her father's
lands, and, t)n the death of her mother in 129(5, of those also held hy her
in jointure.

This John Ap-Adam had summons to Parliament as a Baron from 25
Edw. I. to the 3 of Edw. II. He and Elizabeth his wife had an only son
Thomas, wlio was of age the 18th Edw. II. ; he alienated many of the
estates he had inlierited ; Barew-Cilournay and Beverstone Castle, were sold
by him to Thomas de Berkeley, a great warrior in the reign of Edward III.
Thomas Ap-Adam married Johanna Basset, and that he died without issue
is shewn hy the rest of the tiefs of his mother Elizabeth reverting to the
other branches of the family of Gournay.

From the Towneley MS., we are enabled to give a
charter of Olivia de Gournay, during her widowhood,
and two of Thomas Ap-Adam's, by which it will be
perceived that Thomas de Gournay of I']ast llarptree,
his relation, was his seneschal. (A))p. CX. Nos. 1, 2, 3.)
Aj)-Adam bore for arms. Argent, on a cross gules
five etoiles or.

I cannot find any account of this family ; hut from
the name I conjecture it was of Welch origin.

Tins Robert de Gournay received as his paternal inlieritance tlie great
maiKjr of ()\(r\vere, on the eoast of Somerset, near Axbridge ; and from
his eliU'r brother, Jolm de Gournay, seventy-two slulHnu-s rent from tlie
manor of Nethcrwere. This '/ik of rent was afterwards (Usjjuted by
Ohvia de Gournay, witUnv of John, in I'J'J'J ; and a hiw-suit of some years'
>tan(HnLC ensued between her and this Rol)ert and Margaret his wife ; but
()h\ia and her lieirs eventually recovered it, in spite of collusive nianai^e-
ment between Robert de (iournay and William de jNIarchia, Rishoj) of
Bath and ^\\■lls. Disputes also arose respecting the manor of Overwere,
which however continued in Robert and his descendants.*

Robert de Gournay and Margaret his wife had issue Anselm de
Gournay.

Anselm de Gournay, of Overwere, called '' Le Pere," defended his right
the 8th of Edw. II. J 31 5, to four messuages and a shop in the suburbs of
Bristol without the Newgate, against John de la Rolje and Alice his wife.''
This xVnselm de Gournay le Pere and Thomas de Gournay, with John
parson of the church of Langebigge and others, were accused in the
8th Edw. II. of taking away by violence from tiie lands of Roger Leger
at Langebigge, who was at the time a prisoner amongst the Scots, three
horses, twelve oxen, six cows, two-hundred -^beep, and sixty pigs, of the
value of £50 ; also, in the like way, goods ai-d chattels of the value of
i'lOO ; and therefore the writ directs the sherifi' of Somersetshire to ex-
amine into the truth by the inquisition of honest men.—Tested at Rame-
seye, 3d day of November.

The original is copied at full length (App. CXI.)

This is a curious instance of the lawless state of society at this period,
in which a Baron and his relation, and the parson of the parish, would com-

Sir Matthew dv Cuurnay married Alice Heanchainp the graud-dauuliter of

fiMjutier, tlie family of Mortimer posM-sed great iiiHuei.ee in all the
iiei^hlumriiig counties.

Sir 'riiomas de- (jouriiay was a man of a savage and cruel disposition,
and a strong i)arti/.an of Uueeii Isabella and the Mortimers, in opposition
to iMlward II. and was intrusted by thai princess, in conjunction with Jiilin
Maltravcrs and William de Ogle, with the custod\' of her unfortunate
husl)and.^

'I'iie King at first had been delivered into the h.aiids of the Earl of
Leicester, whose family treated him with loo much lenity. Ciueen Isabella
and her paramour transferred him to the care of these three Kni-hts.
(iournay and Maltravers conducted him from Kenilworth to Corfe Castle,
then to Bristol, where the citizens shewing some disposition to send him
abroad, his keepers removed him in the night to Berkeley Castle.

Thomas Lord Berkeley was ordered to receive him there by indenture
of the Earl of Lancaster (brother of that earl who was beheaded after the
Battle of Pomfrel), having an allowance of 5/. a day for his expenses ; but
the Lord Berkeley was accused of treating the deposed monarch with too
much c;onsideration; whereuptm that nolileman retired to Bradley, one of
his manor houses.''

(Iournay and Maltravers were now Ids sole keepers, and they exercised
towards him every sort of indignity, under the directicjn of the (iiieen and
her minister Adam de Orleton Bishop of Ilereforil, in the hope of
destroying him by their cruel ami insulting treatment. They caused him
to ride thinly clad, and with uncovered head, that the severity of the
weather might affect him. They prcventei' him from sleeping, gave him
unwdtolesome food, and contradicted a. his wishes, in order that liy
watchings, cold, and mortifications, they might hurry him to a jiremature
grave. Pursuing this plan of crafty villaiiy they made him a crown of
straw, and ironically saluted him " l^ire forth, Sir King." To avoiil
meeting any of his friends, they turned towards the marshes of the Severn,

A.n.   1327.] MURDER OF EDWARD II. 015

and to i)rcvcnt liis l)eing rccoLniiscd resolved that his head shoidd be
sliavi'd, hrinuing some dirty water out of a nei^hljo\irii)g diteli for tlie
jjurpose. The K'ing weepiiii; profusely at this iii(lii:,iiit}' cxelaiiiied " that
he would have warm water, altliouiz;h from his own tears."

The wretehed King secured at Berkeley, his unworthy (iueen renewed
her consultations with the Bisho]) of Hereford. Their scheme of des-
troying him by afflictions and ])rivations not having succeeded, the safi'ty
not only of themselves but of many others was now involved, and
demanded the death of Edward, whose protracted life was contein])lated
with all the impatience of alarmed and con.seious guih.

Reproachful letters were sent to his gaolers for behaving towards him
with too much delicacy. And the Bishop of Hereford, knowing that they
dared not jjroceed to the last extremity without a written authority, sent
tluin an ambiguous order, couched in the following words '■ lulwardum
occidere nolite timere bonum est," which may he translated according to
the punctuation, either " Fear not to kill Edward, it is a good thing;" or
" Do not kill Edward, it is good to fear it."

The order was interpreted as was intended, and the inhuman wretches
Gournay and INhdtravers shut their sovereign uj) in a loathsome chamber,
imagining the fetid exhalations would destroy him, but the King reaching
a window cried out to some carpenters who were working near.

They n(nv found that nothing but actual miu-der wouhl avail; and one
night whilst he was in bed they rushed U])on him, half suffocated him
with bolsters, and thrust a red hot iron into his bowels through a horn, in
order that no external mark might ajjpear. The wretched Edward
screamed in his agonies till some in the castle heard him, who, suspecting
the catastrophe, began to pray for his dejjarting soul; but ■ j one dared to
interfere, and he expired in torture unrelieved.

the King, heing at York, delivered instructions to a ' night of that county.

1333 the year of Thomas de Gournay's death to AVinchester." Mal-

After the peace of Britany, France was ravaged by bands of military
adventurers called Free C,'omi)anies ; these were collected together, and
headed by the celebrated French comniamler Du Guesclin, by Matthew de
Gournay, and others, both French and English, who were weary of a state
of peace and the inertness it produced. They led these bands into Spain,
for the purpose of placing Henry Trastamare upon the throne of Castille.'^
Peter the Cruel, half brother of Henry Trastamare, was driven from his
kingdom by Du Chiesclin and his followers, and tied to the Black Prince
into Gascony, who undertook to restore him to bis donunions. The ])rince
innnediately sent his heralds into Spain, ordering all English and (iascon
knights and their dependants to return from the army of Du (iuesclin ; upon
which Sir Matthew de Gournay, Sir Eustace d'Ambreticourt, Sir John
Devereux, and others, innnediately took leave of Henry King of Ca^tille in
the most courteous manner they could, without discovering either their
(nvn or their ])rince's intentions. King Henry made them very handsome
presents, thanking them most gratefully for their services; after whii'h
they left Spain and returned to Gascony as speedily as jjossible.'

The Prince of Wales marched into Spain with the deposed King Peter
the Cruel, and gained the great and decisive victory of Xajara, over Hcin-y

The Karl of Cambridge and his followers jiroceeded to England after
these events. This is the last mention of Sir .Matthew de Gournay in the
wars of the period in which he lived ; but he occurs perpetually in the
different records.

\Ve h.ave seen that he acquired considerable estates in Gascony.'' In his
native county of Somerset the inheritance of his brothers devolved upon
him ;" to these estates he added others; some of them probably by
purchase of the heirs of John last Lord Beauchamp of Hache, whose widow
he married. Stoke-under-IIampden, where he resided in liis old age, had
been among the castles of the Lords Beauchamp, and was included in these
purchases; also Shepton Mallet; and by his second marriage with the
sister of Lord Talbot, of Rieard's Castle, he obtained part of the manor of
^Voodham Mortimer, in Essex.''

There is a curious deed of sale or promissory note from John Lord
Beauchamp of llache to Matthew Gournay, whereby he agrees to pay two
thousand pounds sterling, at the Feast of St. Michael the Archangel, for
the said Matthew's wool; the purchaser calls himself John de Beauchamp
of the county of Somerset, Knight, Lord of llache, merchant (mercator) of
the county of Somerset; it is sealed with his arms.

joyned to another, '3 of menne harneshid and shildid, and •_> of women.

l)art of the ehapelle also -J tumbes without images.

SON  OF KVA  DK  (iOUKN'AY  AM) THOMAS   DlC  llAIU'TUEK.

Hugh dk (ioruxAY of Harpetree is \)h\CA'd m many ancient pedigrees
as the yuuui^^er Ijrotlier of the llegieide.

It seems however, from a MS. in possession of Sergeant Ludlow, that
Rohert de (iournay, son of Rva and Thomas de Ha^.tree, had a youn-er
hrotlier, Hugh,"uiio married Lueia,^ widow of Rohert de Berkeley, and had
issue Hugh de Gournay of llarptree. This Hugh de Gouvnay the hrother
of Robert appears to have been confounded by Dugdale with the last
Anglo-Norman baron with whom he was cotemporary. . It is obvious that
the hunting in Bristol forest and being at the tournament at Blythc refer
to this Hugh, brother of Robert, as the estates forfeited were in the
counties of Gloucester, Leicester, and Warwick ; whereas the estates of
the Anglo-Norman barons were not in those counties. "^

This Hugh de Gournay of Harptree held lands in East Harptree in the
•25lh Edw. L 1297, as is shewn by a tine between him and John Ap-Adam,
respecting the pounding of some cattle.^ He married Joan, and was dead
4 ImIw. hi. 13;j1 ; and by settlement of land in Radewyk and other places
it appears that he had an elder son Thomas, of whom hereafter, and a
second, Hugh.' We conceive that tlie letters of safe conduct granted to
a Hugh de tJournay abroad on the King's service, 12 Edw. HI., relate to
this second son.'

We have also found a mention of Richard de Gournaye of Harptree in
the 28th Edw. 111., who might be another son of the elder Hujih.

I have now coinpk'tcd my account of the Gournays of Somersetshire,
all the In-anchcri vi' which race ajipear to have hecome extinct at the
periods and in the way I have mentioned. It is pussihle h(jwever that
otf.-hoots from the ])arent tree may have existed, and which have not
fallen under my notice. I am induced to think that the more likely, from
my having seen an original letter (of which I give a co])y in App.
C;X^'11I.) written in \6Sb hy the Rev. Justinian Gournay, Rector of
^Va(lenllo iu Northamptonshire ; to which is attached a seal much Ijroken :
but that it was an escutcheon of arms, jjuly of six pieces, is distinctly
visible; also the crest, a head out of a ducal coronet. The letter itself
contains a proposal of marriage of Mr. Justinian Gournay to "Madam
Freeman at her house in Wellingborough," and is a comical example of
the cpiaint formality of such transactions at the penod it was written.

Of the manors uf Inglifthcomlje ami Bai'ew Gurney, whicli were the
most ancient possessions of the Somersetsliire hrancli of the (iournays
(having hekjnged to theni at the time of the Survey), the former appears
to have heen forfeited at the attainder of Thomas de Gournay the Regicide,
and never to have heen restored. Barew Gurney was scjhl hy Thomas
Ap-Adani to Thomas Lord Berkeley and Margaret his wife, the (ith Edw.

of Gore, and now helongs to Mr. Gore Langton. The old mansion of

date when the manor was held hy the Gournays, and I therefore introduce
a view of it, and of the Church of Barew, which is innnediately near it.

No. :].-—^L■\NEulu.M (page 27'J).

.Manors were so called from being residences. What we now call manors (i. e. sokes, vills, or
lord^hipb) did not acipiire that title until a comparatively recent peri<jd. .\s late as the time of
Edward HL the term inaitmiuin was employed only to designate a mansion, which in fact is the
same thing.-This remark may help in other inquiries where, the term iiKLnerium being found,
no manor aiH)i'ars in the usual arceptation of the word.

I have stated, at page 378, that Edmund (jurnay was one of the standing council in the nature
of recorder of the city of Norwich ; tlie extracts above prove that he stood in the same relation
with regard to the borough of Lynn, and that his retaining fee (which occurs several times) was
forty sliillings a year. We find Thomas .Morton going on belialf of the cor|)oration to West
liarsliam, to consult Ediiiund tnirnay in consequence of the rebellion (riot) occasioned by Pinhp
Wyth. Edmund (.Jurnay and Kichard de Walton, the justices, came to Lynn in consequence,
and iield a sessions fur the purpose of pronouncing sentence of puuishmeut U])on Philip \Vythe.
Tiiii, however, docs not appear to have been suflicicnt, as .Viidrew Cavendish, slierilf of Norfolk,
was called in to inquire into the matter, in conjunction with Edmund Ciuniay and other justic.-s.
Who tliis I'hifqi Wyliie was does not appear; but it is likely he was an agent, perliaps baililV, of
tlie Uishop of Nor«icii, at that time superior lord of the borough of Lynn, and lietween whom
■ and the connmmitas, or corporation, a continued contest of rights was carried on.

Tlie riglit- of tlie Bishop of Norwich were granted to the corporation of Lynn by Henry \'II1.

WiLLi.\.M GuitNEY IV. (page 399)
This William Gurney's name appears in the commission of array for the county of Norfolk,
dated 8th Dee. L>nd Richard III. (1484). 'i'his was eight months before the battle of Bosworth
Field, which took place Aug. '2-2, 1485. Whether William Gurney was there does not appear,
but his being in the coinniission of array under Richard III. proves him to have been a Yorkist,
which is continned by the fact of his having been escheator for Norfolk under Edward IV. The
Yorkists were strong in Norfolk, partly in eonsequence of Anthony W'oodville, brother of Edward
IV. 's queen, hawng married a great Norfolk heiress, the daughter of lord Scales of Middlelon.

No. G.—Family of Fit^R.'VLF (page 439).
I have stated that the family of FitzRalf was formerly called de Pebencrs, from a manor held

## p. 707 (#782) ############################################

707

edigree at page IdO. According to Morant this pcdigrco seems correct; but Elizabeth Titz-
talph, wliowa= heiress of this family, was danghter of John FitzUalph and Alice Waleshorongh ;
he married Sir Kobert Chamberlayn, and the manor of Pebeners, as well as some of the N'.irf.dk
states which the I'luKalphs inherited from the Mortimers, passed to her descendants.* I have
lentioned that Maude FitzKalf, the sister of John FitzUalf, who married Alice Walesborough,
ertaiidy inherited estates of the FitzUalfs, derived from Mortimer ; this she did by settlement,
s appear^ from the folluwing memorandum taken from MS. Ilarl. 970, p. 14.

"Memorand. That the said John I'ltzltalV, Esq. sun of the said John FitzRatT, Kt. and
ither of tlie said John FitzHalf, Esq. in consideration of a marriage had between John his son
nd one Alice Walishbury (nearly allied to the Countess of Sulf. at the will and request of Will.
I. of SulF. and the said Countess) caused a state of the said manor of Skoulton, .Mag. Elingham,
nd Potington, among others, to be made to llichd. the E. of M'arwick, Joh. Howard, and others,
ri fee to the use of himself and of Julian, then his wife, and the heirs males of their two bodies,
lem'". to the heirs males of the body of the said John FitzRaff. Uem'''^^''. to IMaiid sister to the
aid Juhn F. the son, and to her heirs in fee. John FitzUalph Esq. and Julian his wife, after
lied, and J. FitzKalph, their son, died without issue male. l!y rea.,on wheruf the right and
itle to the premises remained unto the said Maud, the bister to the said John. liy reason
vlierof the said Robert Colliers and Maud his \Yife entered into the said ])remises and took the
ssucs and profits thereof. And the said Maud died thereof seized ; after whose death the
n-emises descended unto John Coniers, as sou and heir of the said .Maud, who entered and thereof
vas seized, ami died thereof so seized. After whose death the premises descended to the said
F.le Eovell and .Vnu Spelman, according to the form of the pedigree aforesaid."

According to this, the descendants of .Maud FitzRalf liad no right to quarter the arms of that
amily, as Elizabeth I'itzRalf, her niece, who married Sir Robert Chamberlain, was the real
leiress in blood of the family of FitzRalf; nevertheless, the descendants of Maud FitzRalf,
vho by settlement inherited the estates named in the memorandum, did quarter the arms of Fitz-
italf, and, through that family, those of Mortimer of Attleboro', as appears in the arms of the
3urneys of West IJarsham, who were amongst the descendants of Maud FitzRalf. This looks
IS if the right of ipiarteriug arms anciently depended upon inheritance of land as well as upon
■epresenting the line of a family.

In the churih at Pebmarsh is a monumental effigy of one of the FitzRalfs, of which 1 have
nserted an engraving at page 440.

No 8.—Lewk.sou Family (page 4G2).
1 liave pointed out the fact of the small anioimt of land under cultivation possessed by the
Lcwknors, a knightly family, in the time of Henry VII.; and of the enigma it appears how the
gentlemen's families li\edaiid built the manor-houses they did ujjon those few acres. The pro-
habilil\ is, I presume, that from the old mode of cultivation the outlay was less in proportion to
the crop than according to our present mode of dealing with the land. We treat land com-
mercially, looking for a return from cajiital; they treati'd it agriculturally, looking for produce, and
Uviiii/ 111 return for time and labour.

A loarncJ friend of mine rominds inc that the moiiey-chaiip-ers in the temple at .JenijaU'iu
iim=l have been hankers of a Certain sort in the time of our Savionr. He i-, of upmion lliat m
very early tnne, tiu- temples of the Coiii «ere the plaee, v.h.re money wa^ left on il.pu-il iu the
lianiis of tlie priests, at interest, and by tliem lent out, the sacred character of ihrir olfue
causing them to obtain credit, and, at the same time, power to olitain repayment of h.ans. He
thinks he detects this custom in the 21st book of the Iliad, where Neptune and Apollo (that i^,
the priests of their temples) are said to liave lent money at interest to I.aomedon, Kujtr of 'I'roy,
iii order to enable liim to build the walls of that city.     The passage is as follows :—

To be translated thus :—

" You simple fellow ! what an irrational head you have, and a memory which forgets all the
ill usage which we two have suffered at the hands of the Trojans ! we only of all the gods
when, having permission of Jupiter, we lent the monej/ upon a dtclnred j'er-centage per aim
I.aomedon signed the contract. Upon this I furnished the cash for building the walls of Troy
most spacious and strong, such as never to be taken by a hostile force. But you, Apollo, furnisliet
the oxen for carriage of materials, and kept them in four pastures, on Mount Ida. I'jut, when th(
term arrived fur payment of the interest, then he (Laomcdon) withheld the whole, brutally
insulting us, and driving us with threats from his presence. As to you, he threatened to bind
you hand and foot, and transport you to a desert and far distant island; and he vowed that lie
would next cut off the ears of us both."
